I just get… overwhelmed, I guess, if I feel like I didn’t stick to what I planned.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that. I mean, I know what you’re asking, obviously, but it feels like such a harsh way to say it.\n\nSometimes after eating I just get really uncomfortable and I want the feeling to go away, but… I don’t know. Bathrooms in the dorm are also so gross, like there’s always someone in there doing their skincare routine for twenty minutes, so it’s not exactly private. Sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I guess I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise to try to burn off calories after eating?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I’d put it exactly like that. Like “burn off calories” sounds so… direct. I do think about walking places instead of taking the shuttle sometimes, but also the shuttle is always packed and smells weird, so that’s not really— sorry.\n\nAfter eating, I can get kind of restless, but I don’t really track it in that way. I guess I’m not sure what counts as exercising for that reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to try to lose weight or counteract the effects of eating?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know. I mean, I know what laxatives are, like my grandma used to keep this huge bottle in the bathroom cabinet and it always had this scary warning label on it, which I remember because I used to read labels when I was bored as a kid.\n\nBut for me… I don’t think of it that way. Diuretics, I’m not even totally sure what counts? Like water pills? Sorry, I’m kind of getting off track. I guess I haven’t really noticed using something like that for eating, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're eating much more quickly than usual during these times when you feel like you've eaten too much?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really paid attention to the speed, I guess. Like sometimes I’m more focused on how it feels afterward than how fast I was eating.\n\nAlso dining hall meals are weird because everyone’s kind of rushing anyway, like people are trying to get to class or they’re standing around with trays and it makes me feel watched, even if they’re probably not. So I don’t know what “much more quickly than usual” would even be for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're eating an amount of food that's definitely larger than what most people would eat under similar circumstances?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to compare that. Like, “most people” feels hard because I don’t know what people actually eat when they’re not in the dining hall, you know? People’s plates look different and then some people go back for more and some people don’t, and I end up noticing weird things like whether they took two napkins or one, which is not the point.\n\nI guess sometimes it feels like too much to me, but I don’t know if it would be definitely larger than what someone else would eat. I kind of lose the comparison part.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've eaten an amount of food that you couldn't stop or control?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it “couldn’t stop.” Like… I can usually stop, I think. Or I stop because the situation ends, like my friend is done eating or I have to leave or the dining hall starts clearing out, which makes me feel weird because the chairs scrape really loudly and everyone suddenly looks busy.\n\nBut in the moment, I don’t know. It can feel kind of blurry, like I’m not making a clean decision exactly. Sorry, that probably doesn’t answer it. I haven’t really noticed it as, like, “I had no control.”\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you eat much more than you planned during a short time, even if it's not necessarily a large amount of food to others?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I guess “planned” is hard because I don’t always have, like, an actual plan written out or anything. Sometimes I’ll think I’m just going to have one thing and then I have something else too, but then I don’t know if that counts or if that’s just normal.\n\nAnd “short time” is weird too, because in the dining hall it all feels short, like you sit down and then suddenly everyone’s leaving and someone is wiping tables near you even though you’re still there. So I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed it in a clear way, like “oh, I ate way more than I meant to.” It’s more just… afterward I start thinking about it too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've eaten an amount of food that you think is more than most people would eat in a similar situation, even if it doesn't feel like a lot to you at the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say that clearly. Like, sometimes I’ll look at someone else’s tray and think, oh, they have basically the same things, but then I start noticing if they’re eating fast or if they leave crumbs or if they get a drink, and then I’m not really answering the question anymore.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t had a moment where I’m like, “that was definitely more than most people would eat.” It’s more that I get stuck comparing, and then I kind of lose track of what the comparison even was.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight influences what you choose to eat or how much you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think so. I don’t always decide it in a straightforward way, but it’s there. Like even the idea of gaining one pound makes me panic, which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud.\n\nIf I eat what people would probably call a normal dinner, or if something has butter in it, I start thinking everyone will somehow be able to tell. Like they’ll see that I lost control or lost my discipline. And then I start changing what I pick, or how much, even if I try to act like I’m just not hungry or I don’t like that food.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits are more about avoiding weight gain or about feeling in control of your food intake?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s both, but the weight thing is what starts it. Like, if I even imagine gaining a pound, I get this awful panic feeling, and then I start trying to control everything because it feels like the only way to stop that from happening.\n\nBut then it turns into being about discipline too, if that makes sense. Like if I eat a normal dinner, or something with butter in it, I’ll think people can somehow see it on me right away, like they’ll know I messed up or didn’t have enough control. Which is kind of ridiculous, because obviously people aren’t staring that closely, but it feels real in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Is your decision to limit your food intake more about avoiding weight gain or feeling in control over what you eat?\n\nPatient: I think it starts with avoiding weight gain. Like even the idea of gaining a pound makes me panic, and then I start trying to control everything I eat because it feels safer.\n\nBut then it gets tangled up with discipline, too. Like if I eat a normal dinner, or something with butter in it, I feel like everyone will somehow be able to see it on me, like they’ll know I lost control. Which I know doesn’t really make sense, but it feels really obvious to me at the time.\n\nDoctor: Is feeling in control of your food intake more important to you than avoiding weight gain?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can separate them that neatly. Like, feeling in control matters a lot, but it’s mostly because I’m scared of what happens if I’m not in control.\n\nEven gaining one pound feels… terrifying. Like if I eat a normal dinner, or something with butter in it, I’ll suddenly feel like everyone can see that I messed up, like I lost my discipline. Which is strange because I know people aren’t actually measuring me with their eyes, but it feels that way.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of circling around it. I guess avoiding weight gain is the thing underneath, and control is how I try to keep it from happening.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is. I mean, I tell myself it’s about being disciplined or making “good choices,” and then I’ll get distracted thinking about, like, what I ate last Tuesday or whether I should’ve picked a different thing at the dining hall—but underneath it, it’s really the weight.\n\nEven the idea of gaining one pound makes me panic. Like if I eat a normal dinner, or something with butter in it, I feel like everyone will be able to see it on me somehow, like they’ll know I lost control or didn’t have enough discipline. I know that probably sounds irrational, but in the moment it feels really real.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

And then I start changing what I pick, or how much, even if I try to act like I’m just not hungry or I don’t like that food.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits are more about avoiding weight gain or about feeling in control of your food intake?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s both, but the weight thing is what starts it. Like, if I even imagine gaining a pound, I get this awful panic feeling, and then I start trying to control everything because it feels like the only way to stop that from happening.\n\nBut then it turns into being about discipline too, if that makes sense. Like if I eat a normal dinner, or something with butter in it, I’ll think people can somehow see it on me right away, like they’ll know I messed up or didn’t have enough control. Which is kind of ridiculous, because obviously people aren’t staring that closely, but it feels real in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Is your decision to limit your food intake more about avoiding weight gain or feeling in control over what you eat?\n\nPatient: I think it starts with avoiding weight gain. Like even the idea of gaining a pound makes me panic, and then I start trying to control everything I eat because it feels safer.\n\nBut then it gets tangled up with discipline, too. Like if I eat a normal dinner, or something with butter in it, I feel like everyone will somehow be able to see it on me, like they’ll know I lost control. Which I know doesn’t really make sense, but it feels really obvious to me at the time.\n\nDoctor: Is feeling in control of your food intake more important to you than avoiding weight gain?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can separate them that neatly. Like, feeling in control matters a lot, but it’s mostly because I’m scared of what happens if I’m not in control.\n\nEven gaining one pound feels… terrifying. Like if I eat a normal dinner, or something with butter in it, I’ll suddenly feel like everyone can see that I messed up, like I lost my discipline. Which is strange because I know people aren’t actually measuring me with their eyes, but it feels that way.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of circling around it. I guess avoiding weight gain is the thing underneath, and control is how I try to keep it from happening.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is. I mean, I tell myself it’s about being disciplined or making “good choices,” and then I’ll get distracted thinking about, like, what I ate last Tuesday or whether I should’ve picked a different thing at the dining hall—but underneath it, it’s really the weight.\n\nEven the idea of gaining one pound makes me panic. Like if I eat a normal dinner, or something with butter in it, I feel like everyone will be able to see it on me somehow, like they’ll know I lost control or didn’t have enough discipline. I know that probably sounds irrational, but in the moment it feels really real.\n\nDoctor: Do you currently weigh less than 85% of the expected weight for your age and height?\n\nPatient: I don’t know the exact percentage. Student health is the one who said it’s too low—they sent me here because they said my weight wasn’t high enough to clear me for the youth workshops.\n\nThe number has been going down, I know that, but… I guess I don’t think of it in percentages. I’ve been trying to keep up with everything, and those workshops have all these forms and requirements, and it’s honestly kind of annoying that this is the thing holding it up.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: I mean… yeah, I guess. Not “afraid” like a normal fear exactly, but if I even think about gaining a pound, I kind of panic. Like if I ate a normal dinner, or something with butter in it, I’d just feel like everyone could tell right away that I’d lost control or lost my discipline.\n\nWhich sounds dramatic when I say it out loud, but it’s like… I don’t know, people notice things. Even if they say they don’t.\n\nDoctor: Do you limit the types of food you eat or how much you eat in a day?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I guess I do. I sort of plan the whole day around not really eating much. Like coffee before studio, and then if I’m with people maybe I’ll get something from the café and have a few bites so it doesn’t turn into a whole conversation.\n\nI don’t really do bread, or anything oily. It just feels like it would undo the control I’ve been working for, which I know sounds… intense. But it’s hard not to think that way. Also the café near campus puts oil on literally everything, even things that don’t need it, so it’s not like I’m being that unreasonable.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever eat a large amount of food in a short period of time, like within an hour?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… like, “large” is kind of subjective, right? Because some people eat these huge meals like it’s nothing. My roommate can finish a whole burrito and then get chips after, and everyone acts like that’s normal.\n\nFor me, I don’t think of it that way. I mean, sometimes I’ll feel like I ate too much if I had more than I planned, but I don’t know if that’s what you mean by a large amount in an hour. It’s hard to tell.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've lost control while eating?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure… I mean, I don’t usually think of it as “lost control.” It’s more like I get really upset if I eat more than I meant to, even if it probably wouldn’t seem like a lot to someone else.\n\nLike, the other night my roommate was eating cereal out of the box while watching this show, and she didn’t even seem to be thinking about it, which is so weird to me because I’m always thinking about it. But for me it’s not like I black out or anything. I’m aware. I just get… overwhelmed, I guess, if I feel like I didn’t stick to what I planned.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever make yourself vomit after eating?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really know how to answer that. I mean, I know what you’re asking, obviously, but it feels like such a harsh way to say it.\n\nSometimes after eating I just get really uncomfortable and I want the feeling to go away, but… I don’t know. Bathrooms in the dorm are also so gross, like there’s always someone in there doing their skincare routine for twenty minutes, so it’s not exactly private. Sorry, that’s not really what you asked. I guess I’m not sure.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever exercise to try to burn off calories after eating?\n\nPatient: I mean… I don’t know if I’d put it exactly like that. Like “burn off calories” sounds so… direct. I do think about walking places instead of taking the shuttle sometimes, but also the shuttle is always packed and smells weird, so that’s not really— sorry.\n\nAfter eating, I can get kind of restless, but I don’t really track it in that way. I guess I’m not sure what counts as exercising for that reason.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever use laxatives or diuretics to try to lose weight or counteract the effects of eating?\n\nPatient: Um… I don’t really know. I mean, I know what laxatives are, like my grandma used to keep this huge bottle in the bathroom cabinet and it always had this scary warning label on it, which I remember because I used to read labels when I was bored as a kid.\n\nBut for me… I don’t think of it that way. Diuretics, I’m not even totally sure what counts? Like water pills? Sorry, I’m kind of getting off track. I guess I haven’t really noticed using something like that for eating, no.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're eating much more quickly than usual during these times when you feel like you've eaten too much?\n\nPatient: I don’t know… I haven’t really paid attention to the speed, I guess. Like sometimes I’m more focused on how it feels afterward than how fast I was eating.\n\nAlso dining hall meals are weird because everyone’s kind of rushing anyway, like people are trying to get to class or they’re standing around with trays and it makes me feel watched, even if they’re probably not. So I don’t know what “much more quickly than usual” would even be for me.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're eating an amount of food that's definitely larger than what most people would eat under similar circumstances?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to compare that. Like, “most people” feels hard because I don’t know what people actually eat when they’re not in the dining hall, you know? People’s plates look different and then some people go back for more and some people don’t, and I end up noticing weird things like whether they took two napkins or one, which is not the point.\n\nI guess sometimes it feels like too much to me, but I don’t know if it would be definitely larger than what someone else would eat. I kind of lose the comparison part.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've eaten an amount of food that you couldn't stop or control?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I’d call it “couldn’t stop.” Like… I can usually stop, I think. Or I stop because the situation ends, like my friend is done eating or I have to leave or the dining hall starts clearing out, which makes me feel weird because the chairs scrape really loudly and everyone suddenly looks busy.\n\nBut in the moment, I don’t know. It can feel kind of blurry, like I’m not making a clean decision exactly. Sorry, that probably doesn’t answer it. I haven’t really noticed it as, like, “I had no control.”\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you eat much more than you planned during a short time, even if it's not necessarily a large amount of food to others?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure. I guess “planned” is hard because I don’t always have, like, an actual plan written out or anything. Sometimes I’ll think I’m just going to have one thing and then I have something else too, but then I don’t know if that counts or if that’s just normal.\n\nAnd “short time” is weird too, because in the dining hall it all feels short, like you sit down and then suddenly everyone’s leaving and someone is wiping tables near you even though you’re still there. So I don’t really know. I haven’t noticed it in a clear way, like “oh, I ate way more than I meant to.” It’s more just… afterward I start thinking about it too much.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you've eaten an amount of food that you think is more than most people would eat in a similar situation, even if it doesn't feel like a lot to you at the time?\n\nPatient: I don’t think I can say that clearly. Like, sometimes I’ll look at someone else’s tray and think, oh, they have basically the same things, but then I start noticing if they’re eating fast or if they leave crumbs or if they get a drink, and then I’m not really answering the question anymore.\n\nSo… I don’t know. I haven’t had a moment where I’m like, “that was definitely more than most people would eat.” It’s more that I get stuck comparing, and then I kind of lose track of what the comparison even was.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of gaining weight influences what you choose to eat or how much you eat?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I think so. I don’t always decide it in a straightforward way, but it’s there. Like even the idea of gaining one pound makes me panic, which sounds dramatic when I say it out loud.\n\nIf I eat what people would probably call a normal dinner, or if something has butter in it, I start thinking everyone will somehow be able to tell. Like they’ll see that I lost control or lost my discipline. And then I start changing what I pick, or how much, even if I try to act like I’m just not hungry or I don’t like that food.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your eating habits are more about avoiding weight gain or about feeling in control of your food intake?\n\nPatient: I think… it’s both, but the weight thing is what starts it. Like, if I even imagine gaining a pound, I get this awful panic feeling, and then I start trying to control everything because it feels like the only way to stop that from happening.\n\nBut then it turns into being about discipline too, if that makes sense. Like if I eat a normal dinner, or something with butter in it, I’ll think people can somehow see it on me right away, like they’ll know I messed up or didn’t have enough control. Which is kind of ridiculous, because obviously people aren’t staring that closely, but it feels real in the moment.\n\nDoctor: Is your decision to limit your food intake more about avoiding weight gain or feeling in control over what you eat?\n\nPatient: I think it starts with avoiding weight gain. Like even the idea of gaining a pound makes me panic, and then I start trying to control everything I eat because it feels safer.\n\nBut then it gets tangled up with discipline, too. Like if I eat a normal dinner, or something with butter in it, I feel like everyone will somehow be able to see it on me, like they’ll know I lost control. Which I know doesn’t really make sense, but it feels really obvious to me at the time.\n\nDoctor: Is feeling in control of your food intake more important to you than avoiding weight gain?\n\nPatient: I don’t know if I can separate them that neatly. Like, feeling in control matters a lot, but it’s mostly because I’m scared of what happens if I’m not in control.\n\nEven gaining one pound feels… terrifying. Like if I eat a normal dinner, or something with butter in it, I’ll suddenly feel like everyone can see that I messed up, like I lost my discipline. Which is strange because I know people aren’t actually measuring me with their eyes, but it feels that way.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of circling around it. I guess avoiding weight gain is the thing underneath, and control is how I try to keep it from happening.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear of gaining weight the main reason you restrict your food intake?\n\nPatient: Yes… I think it is. I mean, I tell myself it’s about being disciplined or making “good choices,” and then I’ll get distracted thinking about, like, what I ate last Tuesday or whether I should’ve picked a different thing at the dining hall—but underneath it, it’s really the weight.\n\nEven the idea of gaining one pound makes me panic. Like if I eat a normal dinner, or something with butter in it, I feel like everyone will be able to see it on me somehow, like they’ll know I lost control or didn’t have enough discipline. I know that probably sounds irrational, but in the moment it feels really real.\n\nDoctor: Do you currently weigh less than 85% of the expected weight for your age and height?\n\nPatient: I don’t know the exact percentage. Student health is the one who said it’s too low—they sent me here because they said my weight wasn’t high enough to clear me for the youth workshops.\n\nThe number has been going down, I know that, but… I guess I don’t think of it in percentages. It just feels like what happens when I’m really focused and busy, like with classes and planning things, and then suddenly everyone else is making it into this big thing.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me your current weight?\n\nPatient: I… I don’t really want to say the exact number. Student health has it written down, though. They sent me here because they said it was too low to clear me for the youth workshops.\n\nIt’s been going down, I know that. But it doesn’t feel like this dramatic thing to me—it just feels like when I’m really focused and busy with everything, classes and planning and all of that, and then suddenly everyone else is acting like the number is the whole story.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Return the JSON now."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{"candidates": ["F50.0", "F50.2"], "note": "", "is_final": false}
